## CLÚSTER C-T13b-28: Sell what's already selling (stick your hand in the money that's already flowing)
- idea: "The best single piece of counterintuitive advice I've ever received": no intentes crear mercados nuevos al principio; vende lo que ya se vende, "stick your hand in the money that's already flowing". Los compradores vuelven a comprar, y tú eres comprador: vende lo que compraste, pero mejor (el único atajo en los negocios), con tu experiencia como diferencia. Perseguir océanos azules es creerse "smarter than Mother Nature": si no hay competencia es porque no hay dinero fluyendo, y la gente no quiere lo que quiere tu ego sino lo que quiere la Madre Naturaleza. Así nació 2 Hour Writer: el nicho de la escritura generaba $2–3M al año en cohortes, y el autor estudió funnels, emails y landing pages (titulares, problemas, estructura de oferta y currículo, garantía, beneficios, FAQs, posicionamiento) sin copiar y pegar; hizo $130.000 en tres días de Black Friday. Todo lo que quieras monetizar ya lo vende alguien: estudia su modelo, mira su monetización con ojos de creador y no de consumidor, y pregúntate si puedes hacerlo mejor. No se limita a lo digital: se puede vender cualquier cosa que ya se venda.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: la cifra del Black Friday varía ($130k en U-007-186/U-001-123; $139k en otros pasajes). - **desarrollo:** "Sell what's already selling": the best piece of counterintuitive advice Dan has received. He created Two Hour Writer because he knew how profitable the writing niche was in his corner of Twitter: people making $2-3 million a year running a cohort on basic writing, not promising money, nothing fancy, with good compelling sales page copy. He writes daily, so he got in; on Black Friday he did $130,000 in sales in three days. To create it he went through their funnels, emails and landing pages and took: headlines, the burning problems they discuss, the offer and curriculum structure, the guarantee, the benefits and the rest ("ethics" in the transcript, likely "etc."), and the positioning (not "how to make money online with writing"). That's inspiration, not copy-and-paste: he has his own way and must articulate it, but borrows the structure and the why behind what works.

# SECCIÓN 26.4 — Poner ojos sobre el contenido


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-22: Get eyes on your content (the algorithm will not save you)
- idea: "The one and only principle of social media growth": tu contenido no significa nada por sí solo; tiene que ponerse frente a gente y ser compartido por audiencias mayores que la tuya ("people, people, people, eyeballs"). El autor lo aprendió con el arte digital (las páginas de curaduría y las community pages le dieron sus primeros ~2,000 seguidores). El algoritmo está hecho de naturaleza humana y psicología (resultado deseado, problema urgente, solución clara): si no te comparten, no escribes tan bien como crees; pero puedes controlar cuánta gente ve tu contenido con esfuerzo manual (responder a cuentas grandes y pequeñas, DMs, etiquetar en hilos). Crecer es apalancarse en quien ya tiene la audiencia (en vez de ser "slave to the algorithm"), algo que en el fondo hacen también los anuncios. Ejemplos de distribución sin esfuerzo propio: 50,000 suscriptores en dos semanas por menciones de cuentas mayores; el lanzamiento de Sahil Bloom pidiendo a otros que compartan páginas marcadas de su libro.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-134

- **desarrollo:** With digital art, "this is when I started to understand how to actually grow": "my beautiful images... meant absolutely nothing on their own. I couldn't just post and hope that our Lord and savior the algorithm would save me. I had to get my content in front of people. I had to get my content shared by people with larger audiences than me." "When you zoom out this is the one and only principle for social media growth," even for X/Twitter Spaces or being hosted on someone's podcast: "people people people, eyeballs eyeballs eyeballs."
- **ejemplos:** Twitter Spaces; being a podcast guest.

### U-009-204
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Content element 5 — eyes and effort: the algorithm is human psychology; you can control how many people see your content
- **desarrollo:** Most creators make the massive mistake of caring about the algorithm and latest trends, lacking understanding that the algorithm is based on "human understanding, psychology and mechanics." The principles: a desired outcome, a burning problem, and a clear solution or system. Nail those and people will share; "if they don't share it then you aren't as good of a writer as you think you are" — you need more data, testing and experimentation. Creators realize the algorithm shows content but "don't realize they can control the amount of people that see their content." Put effort into growth, especially as a beginner: reply to larger accounts so their audience sees your replies and follows you; network with creators to help each other; potentially pay for help (detailed in "How to build an audience with zero followers").
- **ejemplos:** Replying to larger accounts.
- **cita:** "if they don't share it then you aren't as good of a writer as you think you are"

That's true to an extent, but "you're missing that all of that is shaped by human nature." Going deeper not only lets you understand it more and get better results, it "opens up this world of depth" that allows you to find meaning in what most people see as superficial — and that's true for many areas of life.

### U-019-018
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Grow by getting content shared by people with audiences (network and persuasion), not by relying on the algorithm
- **desarrollo:** Scenario: you have zero followers, write a post, and someone with 100,000 followers shares it; now your audience on that post approaches 100,000, and further reposts/story shares can push it to hundreds of thousands. This is "a skill". He is not a fan of relying on the algorithm and doesn't teach that; he favors building a network, learning to persuade, and providing value you already have to someone else so they exchange it for their audience. If he reposted one of you to a million people, you'd get his impressions plus everyone in his audience who shares it. Then you promote your newsletter or product to that traffic. Later he asks: when content doesn't go off, are you blaming the algorithm or meeting new people with audiences so you're in control of your growth?
- **ejemplos:** Zero-follower post shared by a 100k account; him reposting someone to one million people.

Two ways: be a slave to the algorithm, or find, network with and potentially pay people who have the audience you want. "That's what you're doing with ads anyways": telling Facebook "target this audience, I'll give you money." "Bypass that, pay less, go to the people that have your audience, have them share your content," attract followers who actually want to read your content, and create them into customers over time; "they don't just disappear after the ad disappears." "You can sell anything to anyone when you create that person because identities are created through information AKA content." Dan dismisses critics: "it doesn't work for them because their brand and content strategy suck." (References his video "How to build an audience with zero followers.")
- **ejemplos:** Facebook ads targeting as paying for someone else's audience.

- **desarrollo:** "The algorithm probably isn't going to make you an overnight success. Most people don't realize that social media growth comes from manual effort and skill"; once you realize it, it's no longer "a game of luck for popular people." You don't need a large audience: "you need a thousand true [fans]" (as transcribed: "a thousand true"); even small audiences make $100,000, $500,000, a million a year. To get traffic without relying on the algorithm: reply to big and small accounts, DM people to make friends and grow together, write threads and tag people so they have a reason to repost. Critics who say "don't pay for growth, don't join an engagement group" often say "if you want to meet smaller accounts, comment on my post," turning their post into an "engagement farm" knowing the small accounts get nothing. "Take matters into your own hands, make friends, call it an engagement group if you want, share each other's stuff": like working on homework with classmates, building projects together, business partners, a marketing guy who helps you grow.

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-23: Idea catalysts (ways to get your posts shared)
- idea: Como no todo se vuelve viral y "there's still such a thing as beginner hell", hace falta un "idea catalyst": formas de poner tus posts ante otras audiencias para crecer y testear ideas. El autor pide dejar el ego ("si no creces, tu contenido probablemente es malo"; el "filósofo loco" que culpa a la audiencia) y aprender estrategia de crecimiento, que es "literally the differentiator". Métodos: (1) networking con 3–5 cuentas pares que comparten estrategias y posts (no engagement groups, sino amigos) o, en la versión de 2024, construir una tribu respondiendo a diario a cuentas de tu tamaño, pasando a DMs y formando un grupo de 4–6 que se comparte; (2) comentar con valor bajo cuentas grandes (Justin Welsh: preguntar a 3–5 líderes cuándo publican y "get there first with value"); (3) hacer un newsletter o video largo tan bueno que se comparta; (4) "pay to play": pagar a individuos con audiencias comprometidas por shares, no a plataformas; (5) intercambiar valor no monetario (shares cruzados entre plataformas, una landing page por 5–10 retweets; "social capital"). Trabajo gratuito como táctica de conexión (un banner regalado).
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: la postura sobre pagar por shares y sobre los engagement groups cambia entre 2022 y 2024 (ver "Evolución y tensiones" y C-T10a-24).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): FUSION: U-001-093 <= U-013-024, U-013-026 | la guía de 2024 reproduce casi literalmente el paso 6 del video de 2022, que en la extracción quedó en dos unidades (dejar el ego y aprender crecimiento; cuatro formas de conseguir shares); se conserva la versión que integra ambas

### U-001-093
- **tipo:** proceso
- **titulo:** Writing business step 6: learn to grow on a platform — branding plus getting eyes on your profile via shares, networking, comments, great newsletters, and paying for shares.
- **desarrollo:** Many think they're too good to learn social media growth strategy, yet it's "literally the differentiator" between doing what you love for a living or not. Set ego aside: if you're not growing, your content probably sucks; the "crazy philosopher" who blames the audience has no idea what people want or what an impactful message is. Learn (1) branding — what makes a compelling profile people want to follow (human nature, psychology, marketing) — and (2) getting eyes on your profile, since the best profile and content won't grow without eyeballs. The most effective way: people sharing your content with their audience (retweet, story share, Facebook share, YouTube share, newsletter shares). Methods: (a) networking with similar accounts — he isn't a big fan of engagement groups but is of making friends, a group of three to five close online friends who share strategies and each other's posts, since offline friends probably aren't doing this; (b) commenting on big accounts' posts with value and attention-catching comments (practice writing) so people click and follow if your profile is good; (c) making your newsletter so good it gains traction and gets shared, like a great long YouTube video; (d) paying for shares — "influencer marketing," not paid ads/boosts: pay someone to share your content to their audience; he prefers paid shares over shoutouts.
- **ejemplos:** 3–5 person online friend group; valuable comments on big accounts.
- **cita:** "set your ego aside because it's not just posting good content"
- **terminos:** branding; eyes on your profile; engagement groups; influencer marketing; paying for shares
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-001-092
- **fuente:** The One-Person Business Model (How To Productize Yourself Full Guide).md, 1:55:40–1:58:52, 2024-02-06

### U-013-024
- **tipo:** advertencia
- **titulo:** Step 6: set your ego aside and learn platform growth — if you aren't growing, your content probably sucks
- **desarrollo:** Too many people think they're too good to learn social media growth strategies, when it is "literally the differentiator between whether you do what you love for a living or not." It's not just posting good content: "if you aren't growing, one, your content probably sucks." Good content is a given. The trap: "I'm this crazy philosopher, I write things so clever, but nobody likes it, it's a problem with them" — "no dude, you have no idea what people want and you have no idea what an impactful message consists of."
- **ejemplos:** The "crazy philosopher" writer who blames the audience.

### U-013-026
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Four ways to get shares: network with 3-5 peer accounts, comment on big accounts, make long-form so good it spreads, pay for shares
- **desarrollo:** (a) Networking with similar accounts: he is "not a huge fan of engagement groups," but is a fan of making friends — a group of three to five close online friends you grow with, sharing strategies and each other's posts, "because your offline friends probably aren't doing this, so you need a new friend group." (b) Commenting on big accounts' posts: they have a large audience; a good comment that delivers value and catches attention (where you practice your writing skills) gets people to click your profile, and if it's good they follow. (c) Make your newsletter (or long YouTube video) so good it gains traction and people share it with friends — you build loyal followers from the start. (d) "A less taboo way than you think": paying for shares — not paid ads (Facebook ads, Instagram/Twitter boosts) but influencer marketing: you pay someone to share your content to their audience; if they like it they follow. He doesn't like shout-outs; he prefers paying for shares.
- **ejemplos:** Engagement groups vs. friend group of 3-5; influencer marketing.
- **cita:** "i don't like shout outs i like just pay someone for shares"
- **terminos:** networking, engagement groups, influencer marketing, paying for shares
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio

- **desarrollo:** Not all posts go viral and "there's still such a thing as beginner hell": you must get over a hump of followers and need a way to test whether ideas are good. The "idea catalyst" gets your posts in front of other audiences. Three ways follow: pay to play, building a tribe, exchanging non-monetary value.

### U-014-163
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Catalyst 1, pay to play: pay individuals with engaged audiences (paid shares) rather than platforms
- **desarrollo:** Ads pay Facebook or Google to show your post to their audience; he'd rather pay an individual with a highly engaged audience with whom he wants a relationship. Most people with an audience offer growth services or promo spots. Being shared by a larger account for pay is "on par with paid ads and paid sponsorships" (like podcast or newsletter sponsorship) — you're not asking anyone to buy, just testing if content does well and attracts followers. Best way: DM accounts over 20,000 followers and ask if they help with growth or offer promotion. Big brands/large accounts often don't check DMs (not spending 8-12 hours a day on 300 DMs) and charge a lot because audiences are valuable — some charge $10,000 for 10 shares. So an audience alone is valuable even without a product. If you won't invest: fine, but "I'd question whether you're serious about building your life's work" — you'll spend $40 on a dinner but not on traffic that could make 10x with a product and ROI. Benefit vs paid ads: you keep part of the audience (follows, newsletter) and drive product traffic. Those who dismiss it as inauthentic or not working "don't know what you're doing." He hasn't done it in three years and avoids teaching it, but did it when escaping freelancing; "you don't need to do that."
- **ejemplos:** $10,000 for 10 shares; $40 dinner vs traffic.
- **cita:** "I'd rather pay an individual with whom I want to build a relationship"
- **terminos:** pay to play, paid shares, ROI
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** avanzado
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-162
- **fuente:** How Smart Creators Will Grow An Audience From Zero In 2025.md, 25:33-29:00, 2024-10-20
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-014-164
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Catalyst 2, building a tribe: reply daily to accounts your size, move to DMs, form a share group
- **desarrollo:** Do this whether or not you pay. It's not an engagement pod you're tossed into with people you don't care about; you're injecting yourself into a social media tribe. Find accounts around your size, add them to a list or bookmark profiles, reply to their content daily with your own insights, stories, anecdotes (not regurgitating or "great post") — start real conversations. If not naturally social, research social dynamics and how to hold conversations, online. Move to DMs; ask what they're doing to grow ("hey dude I'm trying to grow just the same as you"); they'll likely share as a friend; then position: "if I send you a thread you can send me one back and we'll both share it." With 4-6 people you have a group: send an idea, they share it — more viral potential, traffic, followers. Consistency reveals "levers that you can pull for audience growth."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "you're trying to inject yourself into a social media tribe"
- **terminos:** tribe, engagement pod, levers
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-014-162
- **fuente:** How Smart Creators Will Grow An Audience From Zero In 2025.md, 29:00-30:22, 2024-10-20

- **desarrollo:** Exchange value not in money: if you have a big Instagram and I have a big Twitter, "I'll share you on Instagram if you share me on Twitter." Or offer a service/product to a larger account: "I'll create a landing page for you or write an email for you if you give me 5 to 10 retweets or shares." "Social capital and trading goods is just as viable as trading money."
- **ejemplos:** Instagram-for-Twitter share swap; landing page for 5-10 retweets.

### U-005-024
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Early traction tactic #1: ask 3-5 favorite thought leaders when they post, then get under their posts first with value.
- **desarrollo:** The first of "two really simple ways" to get initial traction: "being underneath large accounts," similar to Twitter. Because LinkedIn "is run by Microsoft and therefore antiquated and doesn't roll out very good features," there is no notification bell for someone's posts. So: (1) find three to five favorite thought leaders; (2) message them: "when do you post? I don't want to miss it."; (3) whenever they post, "get there first"; (4) "But get there first with value... Add something meaningful underneath their posts." Claim: "You can grow from 1,000 followers to 10,000 followers just doing that." Dan contrasts the Twitter version: comment under other people's posts or tag someone with a value post so they retweet it.
- **ejemplos:** Justin publicly giving his own posting time (7:15 a.m. Central) on the show; Dan jokes listeners should expect more engagement then.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-28: Writing is not building an audience (traffic is 80%)
- idea: Escribir contenido no es lo mismo que construir audiencia: la mayoría se queda en escribir y espera a "our Lord and savior the algorithm". Escribir es el 20% de la ecuación y el tráfico el 80%: "there's writing and there's people", tu escritura no se verá sin gente, y la introversión no es excusa. El contenido es una pieza pequeña: importan cuánta gente te conoce y te aprecia, cuánta ve tus posts y la calidad de tus amistades con quienes van en ascenso. El crecimiento tiene dos partes: branding (un perfil convincente) y ojos sobre el perfil, sobre todo por gente que comparte tu contenido; al principio eso se hace a mano, no con el algoritmo.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna (la regla se mantiene de 2022 a 2025; en 2026 se cristaliza en los "two levers", C-T10b-30).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-008-089

- **titulo:** Writing content is not the same as building an audience: get content shared, make your name known, make friends who help you grow
- **desarrollo:** First click before the income streams: you need a brand, writing/creating content and attracting followers — no followers, no one to fuel your business. But "don't necessarily just write content, instead you need to build an audience" — a slight difference. Writing is necessary but most people stop there; they don't put effort into getting content shared, making their name known in the space, or making friends that help them grow. Dan points to a growth-mechanisms video and to Cortex University (an expensive higher-ticket program for serious creators/writers) as a network; you don't have to pay — you can form and find communities online.
- **ejemplos:** Cortex University community.

- **terminos:** build an audience; Cortex University

### U-013-025
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** Platform growth has two parts: branding (a compelling profile) and getting eyes on the profile
- **desarrollo:** You need to understand (1) branding: what makes a good, compelling profile and what makes people want to follow you — "all of this is human nature and human behavior, it's psychology, it's marketing"; and (2) how to get eyes on your profile: you can have the best profile and content but without eyeballs you won't grow. The most effective way to get eyes is people sharing your content with their audience: a retweet, a story share, a Facebook share, a YouTube share, people sharing your newsletter.

### U-002-103

- **titulo:** Step 6 — Learn to network and grow on the platform: get eyes on your content manually at the start
- **desarrollo:** Dan refers to his video "the non-needy networking process" (underviewed, but "one of the most ... actionable and beneficial things you can do"). "You're not going to win ... the social media game if you don't understand the rules and mechanics." Common error he sees in Modern Mastery: "I've been writing content and I'm not getting any followers" — "if you just wrote content and got followers then everyone would be a content creator." You need both good content and eyes on it, and "that is done manually at the start you do not rely on the algorithm." Tactics: readers sharing your writing (because it's good); network with similar-follower accounts and grow together; comment on larger accounts so people see your comment and click to your profile; make your newsletter "so good that people can't help but talk about it"; join a community of people doing the same thing (like Modern Mastery). Not either/or: only good content and hoping won't work; only networking with poor content won't work. "If there's a hole or a problem in your brand fix it."
- **ejemplos:** Modern Mastery students without followers.
- **cita:** "you do not rely on the algorithm you network with people and get them to share your content"

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-31: Traffic mechanisms (replies, quote posts, reposts, manual DMs)
- idea: Los traffic mechanisms son las formas en que la gente ve contenido en cada plataforma; entenderlos junto con la naturaleza humana te da control directo del crecimiento en vez de ser "a slave to the algorithm" persiguiendo la táctica de moda. (1) Replies: el fruto más accesible; los posts aburridos no hacen leer las respuestas, las respuestas aburridas no generan clics (habla de ti y de tu experiencia) y las cuentas pequeñas dan pocas impresiones; responder en masa durante meses hace que te reconozcan ("there's that guy again"). (2) Quote posts: comentario atractivo y dirigido, porque con cuentas gigantes es lotería. (3) Reposts: la gente comparte lo que quiere que su audiencia vea que aprueba; checklist antes de publicar. (4) Manual DMs: lentos pero potentes, sin hablar como un robot corporativo. Para un principiante, "reply like a madman" es parte del sistema.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: "talk about yourself" en las replies (U-004-150, 2023) contrasta con la crítica al contenido "me me me" en la entrevista de 2025 (U-004-016, fuera de este bloque; U-004-021 en C-T10b-19). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-149
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** Understand the traffic mechanisms and human nature, and you're in direct control of growth instead of a slave to the algorithm.
- **desarrollo:** "Traffic mechanisms" = how people get eyes on content on every platform. Understanding them lets you "pick and choose and create a strategy that works best"; "test them all and see what works." "Don't fall for the trap of social media gurus telling you one is any better than the other because the algorithm changed" — "the hot topic of the day is always the algorithm." Dan: "I don't even have to post reels anymore... because I understand human nature... what's going to capture or hold and generate attention. I don't need the algorithm to share my content when I'm in direct control of it." Otherwise you're "a slave to the algorithm, always having to study the latest and greatest tactics and never being able to develop a system or feel to your brand." His Instagram has "an obvious feel"; those posting memes then a carousel then a video show they're "hopping on the bandwagon."
- **ejemplos:** Dan's Instagram brand feel vs. bandwagon accounts.
- **cita:** "I don't need the algorithm to share my content when I'm in direct control of it"
- **terminos:** traffic mechanisms; slave to the algorithm; feel to your brand; direct control
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-144
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 14:31-15:56, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-150
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Traffic mechanism 1, replies — and three things people miss: boring posts don't get replies read, boring replies don't get clicks (talk about yourself), small accounts give few impressions.
- **desarrollo:** Replies are "the most obvious one... low hanging fruit and you have to do it": people read comments, and if yours captures, holds and delivers value on attention, they click your profile and follow. Misses: (1) "boring content doesn't make people read the replies" — a tweet can get huge likes but only 100 people open replies "because there's nothing sparking controversy or conversation"; (2) "boring replies don't make people click on your profile. Stop repeating what was said in the original post. Talk about yourself, tell people how you've experienced the same thing in your life. Talking about yourself leads to the most profile clicks"; (3) "small accounts get fewer impressions overall" — still network with accounts your size, but "be sure you are replying to the bigger accounts with interesting content."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "Talking about yourself leads to the most profile clicks"
- **terminos:** replies; traffic mechanism
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-149
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 15:56-17:24, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** Contrasts with Vitali's advice in the 2025 interview (U-004-016) that "me me me" content fails; here, talking about yourself in replies is recommended because it shows shared experience.


### U-004-151
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** Spam replies over months so your name gets known: "there's that guy again" — and replies with personal stories build trust.
- **desarrollo:** "Not only does replies help you increase your followers, just spam replies, go reply to everyone. You need to get your name in people's mouths... known over the course of months." "This isn't a one-day thing... this is compounding nature where I see someone in the replies multiple times over the course of a month and I'm like oh there's that guy again... maybe I should DM him, maybe I should follow him... oh he's replying to this other guy that I really like too." Creators who spend time in their replies start to notice you. "Things just get better when you reply with a personal story so people trust you more."
- **ejemplos:** "There's that guy again."
- **cita:** "oh there's that guy again there's that guy again maybe I should DM him"
- **terminos:** compounding
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-150
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 16:39-17:24, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-152
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Traffic mechanism 2, quote posts: add engaging commentary (talk about yourself), target accounts that may actually see it — huge accounts make it a lottery.
- **desarrollo:** Quote posts = reposting someone's content with commentary (X's repost/quote; on Instagram, sharing someone's post to your story with commentary and a tag). You write something that captures, holds and delivers value; the creator may see it and repost it, sending traffic. Problem: "you think you can just post something and be like oh this is great" — there's no reason for them to repost, and even if they do (to show their audience validation), "other people aren't going to click on your profile, it's boring." "Again talk about yourself." Nuance: "this one is more like a numbers game and luck" — quote-posting someone with 500,000 followers, they probably won't see it, and if they do "it's just by dumb luck." "If you're going to do some quote posts make sure it's targeted and actually worth your time." Test all mechanisms over 6-12 months.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "if you're going to do some quote posts make sure it's targeted and actually worth your time"
- **terminos:** quote posts; numbers game
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-149
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 17:24-19:30, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-154
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Traffic mechanism 3, reposts: people share what they want their audience to see them agree with — ask before posting "will people share it, and why?"
- **desarrollo:** Reposts (X repost, YouTube/Facebook share, Instagram share to story). "Your content has to be good enough and sharable enough for people to share to their feed." Why people repost — "self-awareness here": "when I repost something it's usually because I want my audience to see what it is that I agree with or resonate with"; "someone's not going to retweet you if they don't want their friends to see what they like." So make it relatable, attention-catching and "something they want to identify with or be known for." Checklist "before hitting post": "will people share it? why should they share it? do I identify with anything I'm saying? is it polarizing enough to spark discussion and get comments and boost engagement further?"
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "someone's not going to retweet you if they don't want their friends to see what they like"
- **terminos:** reposts; sharable
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-149
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 19:30-21:03, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-155
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Traffic mechanism 4, manual DMs: slow but powerful; don't be a corporate robot (the "Zapier automations virtual coffee" anti-example).
- **desarrollo:** "The fourth traffic mechanism is just manual DMs. This one is more difficult and slow but I highly recommend networking in general," also a strategy to get reposts (see the non-needy networking video). "DMs work best when you're not a corporate robot like most people are on LinkedIn." Anti-example: "hey great to connect, I love your stuff, we should have a virtual coffee and talk about the complexity of Zapier automations to enhance the overall experience of your loyal email subscribers so that you can build an incredible digital presence." "You can make friends online to the point where they're happy to just share whatever you ask them to share."
- **ejemplos:** The corporate LinkedIn DM.
- **cita:** "DMs work best when you're not a corporate robot like most people are on LinkedIn"
- **terminos:** manual DMs; corporate robot; non-needy networking
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio

- **tension:** ninguna


### U-027-144
- **tipo:** advertencia
- **titulo:** A beginner's system differs from Koe's: reply like a madman, write shareable content, build a product, then long-form for trust
- **desarrollo:** What he does now won't look the same for an absolute beginner. On social media you'll need to "reply like a madman" to get eyes on your account; practice writing content that gets shared and brings followers; build a product/service that solves your own problems; then write longer-form content to build trust and make sales. "I write 30 minutes every morning" makes no sense without a product. Think big picture: dedicate time to one part, automate it, then shift time to the next, until you build a system that sustains everything.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-32: Followers live in other people's audiences (network leverage)
- idea: Los seguidores que quieres "están siguiendo a otro": la fuente de seguidores son las audiencias ajenas. Si el algoritmo es una lotería, queda un lever manual: que otros compartan tus posts. Tu audiencia real = tus seguidores + los de tu red (500 + 500.000 si alguien te comparte); así los expertos generan tráfico a mano, de forma estratégica y gastando goodwill en el momento justo. El efecto red multiplica el alcance indirecto (1.000 seguidores × 200–500 × 200–500). La diferencia entre una cuenta de 500 y una de 500.000 son las conexiones. Regla práctica: intentar cada semana que alguien con quien conversas comparta uno de tus mejores posts. Con años de escritura y prueba social, puedes escribir a casi cualquiera y obtener respuesta.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-114
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Followers live in other people's audiences: when the algorithm is a lottery, manually get your posts in front of them.
- **desarrollo:** In beginner hell, especially on platforms like Twitter "where the algorithm is kind of non-existent," you can't expect the algorithm to launch you. Dan started there, "so I knew that my growth had to take manual effort," and is "blessed" because "if the algorithm isn't working in my favor, I still have a lever I can pull." Breakdown: "You want followers. Followers come from people seeing your post on their phone and then clicking your profile and being interested enough to follow you." "If the algorithm is like the lottery... how can we manually get people to see your posts? How can we start rolling the snowball...?" "Where is the source of followers or people on social media? And it's in other people's audiences." On social media you can only follow, like, repost, comment (bookmarks etc. "don't matter") — "you engage and you follow. So the followers that you want lie [in] following someone else."
- **ejemplos:** Rolling the snowball into an avalanche.
- **cita:** "Where is the source of followers or people on social media? And it's in other people's audiences"
- **terminos:** lottery; rolling the snowball; other people's audiences

- **nivel:** fundamental

That's how people make tens of thousands with small audiences: they "generate traffic manually through networking." Beginners think they're limited to follower count and the algorithm; experts know they can access their own followers and their network's followers: they write a post they know will bring followers or sales and have 10 to 15 people share it. Not daily — it's strategic: "they build up goodwill and act right when the time is right."

Adjust for engagement rate, content quality and whether people log in. A "very generous" 10,000 people per month somewhat knowing who you are and what you do, multiplied by 6 to 12 months of effort — "I find it extremely hard to fail in this game."

- **desarrollo:** "If you aren't trying to get one of your best posts shared every single week by someone on social media, then you will not grow" — and even if you would, act like you won't. Process: have conversations every week; research the timeline (not scroll/consume); reply, keep and start conversations in DMs; from those conversations you get many content ideas; write a post, thread (usually brings more followers), carousel or reel; send it to the people you discussed the topic with: "hey threw this together thought you'd like it"; you can mention them so they benefit and gain followers, making them more likely to repost.

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-33: Remixing (borrowing the authority and traffic of bigger creators)
- idea: Como los DJs que remezclan canciones populares para "robar su tráfico", los escritores pueden apoyarse en la autoridad y la audiencia de creadores más grandes: citarlos en un thread o newsletter y enviarles el post, comentar su contenido (como los reaction videos), invitarlos a un podcast (que además sirve para hacer networking) y hablar de sus ideas dándoles crédito ("the best idea I've seen this week"). Es ayudar a otros a difundir su nombre para que ellos difundan el tuyo.

(2) "Commentate on their content," like YouTube reaction videos leveraging a larger creator's traffic. (3) "Get people on a podcast because people just love getting on podcasts" — they share it to their audience; the podcast is also a networking tool to meet and befriend bigger people "and ask them for something in the future" — "that's what I did when I had a guest podcast two years ago." (4) "Talk about their ideas and credit them": e.g., "the best idea I've seen this week," explain how it impacted you, credit the author; they may share or comment. This stresses long + short form together; e.g., in a newsletter, "slowly helping other people spread their name so they'll help you spread your name."

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-34: Clippable moments (how the best podcast guests answer)
- idea: Los mejores invitados de podcast no responden la pregunta directamente: enuncian con confianza su mejor idea relacionada y la desarrollan con algunos puntos de apoyo. Eso mantiene al oyente, hace que el podcast rinda, atrae más invitaciones y crea "clippable moments"; si la idea ya fue validada (se volvió viral), el clip volverá a viralizarse. Ejemplo hipotético: Hormozi respondería "cuál es la mayor habilidad" con su tuit sobre mantener el buen humor, un pattern interrupt que además "paga diez veces".
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-022-190
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** The best podcast guests don't answer the question directly: they state their best related idea with confidence, then expand with supporting points.
- **desarrollo:** Studying how guests respond: "the best speakers don't answer the question that the podcast host asks directly. They don't say 'Um, well, good question. Let me think about this for a bit.'… Instead, they just speak their best idea, the best idea that relates to that question, with confidence, and then they expand on it with a few supporting points." Benefits: keeps the listener engaged → the podcast does better → more podcasts want you → more success; it's "a clippable moment," and if the idea has already gone viral/been validated, the clip will again, compounding success.
- **ejemplos:** Alex Hormozi (U-022-191).
- **cita:** "They just speak their best idea… with confidence, and then they expand on it with a few supporting points."
- **terminos:** clippable moment
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-022-185
- **fuente:** How To Articulate Your Thoughts Intelligently (Talk Like This).md [6:17]-[7:01] (2025-12-07)
- **tension:** ninguna

- **tipo:** ejemplo
- **titulo:** Hormozi answers "greatest skill to learn" with his viral tweet about staying in a great mood — a pattern interrupt and a re-viral clip.
- **desarrollo:** Asked "What's the greatest skill that someone can learn right now?", Hormozi could say "learn sales or offer creation," "but he understands that there are levels to this game. So he would probably respond with his literal second most viral tweet: 'The single greatest skill you can develop is the ability to stay in a great mood in the absence of things to be in a great mood about.'" It "has nothing to do with actual skill acquisition that's on people's mind. So that's a pattern interrupt as well." It sets him and the host up "for a very interesting conversation on a topic that he's probably very fresh on and can talk about non-stop." The tweet has 105,000 likes; when clipped it "is going to pay back tenfold because it's going to go viral again." (The author presents this as a hypothetical "would probably respond.")
- **ejemplos:** Hormozi's tweet (105,000 likes).
- **cita:** "The single greatest skill you can develop is the ability to stay in a great mood in the absence of things to be in a great mood about."

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-35: Paid growth (paying for shares of genuinely good posts)
- idea: Pagar a una cuenta grande para que simplemente repostee un post auténtico y bueno (sin anuncio, sin "síguelo") es, para el autor, "10 veces más auténtico" que cualquier otra forma de crecimiento pagado; no se refiere a anuncios para ganar seguidores ni a páginas de shoutouts ("churn and burn"). Las cuentas grandes necesitan un filtro ante cientos de DMs: el pago es ese filtro ("no es injusto, es la realidad"). Opciones: pagar por repost/story share o por una reply (en X las replies te impulsan, en Instagram no); escribir, preguntar, probar y "desperdiciar un poco de dinero". Condición: el buen contenido es prerrequisito; pagar o no da igual porque "solo crecerás si eres bueno". Estas estrategias no son oscuras: para un principiante sin autoridad "es lo que hay que hacer". En 2026 lo sigue validando pero sin apostar por él.
- nivel: avanzado
- evolución: de estrategia recomendada en 2023 (U-004-160) a opción secundaria en 2026 (U-004-115), con prioridad para el networking gratuito. Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-160
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Strategy 3, paid growth: paying a bigger account to simply repost a genuinely good post is "10 times more authentic" than ads or follow-for-follow shoutouts.
- **desarrollo:** "This is where it gets very touchy." People who are "super authentic and moral and noble" with no social media experience brag about not using paid growth; Dan observed them for years — "four years down the road they've either not grown at all or lost followers," becoming "bitter complainers." What he does NOT mean: running ads to gain followers ("the worst thing you can do"; people lose hundreds of followers a day) or Instagram shoutout pages ("I started an Amazon FBA company and grew to a million dollars in 5 seconds... follow account follow account") — it "does not work" for loyal followers; maybe "churn and burn" clients, but no nurturing, no trust. "Just don't do anything that requires something or someone else to tell people to follow you." What he means: "a truly authentic post, one that you would normally write, and using a bit of money to get it in front of other people's eyes." "Good content is obviously a prerequisite"; he tried it at the beginning and it worked "because my content was good." Mechanism: you pay someone you otherwise couldn't reach (they offer it as a service), you network with them, they repost with no ad, no sponsored tag, no "follow him"; people who like it like it, those who want to follow do; you nurture them; eventually they can buy. "To me that's 10 times more authentic than literally any other kind of paid growth" — and better than ads relying on "sleazy direct response principles."
- **ejemplos:** Amazon FBA shoutout-page anti-example; people losing followers after running follower ads.
- **cita:** "just don't do anything that requires something or someone else to tell people to follow you just get eyes on your content"
- **terminos:** paid growth; churn and burn; nurture the audience
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** avanzado
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-149
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 26:31-29:37, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** In 2026 (U-004-115) Dan still endorses paying for shares but says "let's not bank on that," prioritizing free networking.


### U-004-161
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Large accounts need a filter: with hundreds of DMs a day, payment is what earns their attention — "this isn't unfair, it's just reality."
- **desarrollo:** "Reality is harsh: large accounts are busy and probably won't do this, AKA they won't share you unless you pay them because they need a filter." With "hundreds of DMs a day they're not going to spend 24 hours a day responding," they "have other things to do that led to those DMs." "You need to offer payment for them to pay attention to you. If you don't like that, don't start a business; if you don't start a business enjoy the other side which is 10 times more corrupt. This isn't unfair, it's just reality."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "they won't share you unless you pay them because they need a filter"
- **terminos:** filter
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** avanzado
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-160
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 29:37-30:23, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** Matizes the free non-needy networking route (U-004-120), which targets peers and friends rather than very large busy accounts.


### U-004-162
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Paid growth options: pay for a plain repost/story share or a reply (replies boost you on X, not Instagram); DM, ask, test, "waste a bit of money."
- **desarrollo:** Options: ask them to repost you ("just as simple as reposting or sharing to the story, again no caption, no asking to follow you"), or ask them to reply to you — "if you think replies are better then ask them to reply to you for however much money instead." Platform condition: "this really only works on a platform like Twitter or X; on Instagram the replies don't boost you on the other person's feed; on X if someone replies to you their followers still see." Process: "DM them, ask them if they help with growing your brand, take the conversation further and see what they offer... figure it out, test it, waste a bit of money." Objection reply: if it's fine for advertisers to put random products in your face, it's fine for creators to accept money to share content you'll probably like "without you asking to buy anything" — shared content is less positioned to sell than an ad.
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "if you think it's fine for advertisers to promote a product randomly in your face then I don't see why you shouldn't think it's fine for creators to accept money to share content"
- **terminos:** ninguno
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** avanzado
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-161
- **fuente:** How To Build An Audience With Zero Followers (What They Don’t Tell You).md, 30:23-31:52, 2023-10-15
- **tension:** ninguna

- **tipo:** opinión
- **titulo:** These growth strategies aren't dark or inauthentic; for a beginner without authority, "this is what you have to do to grow" — and it changed Dan's life.
- **desarrollo:** "You either learn to play or you don't." "None of this ever in my entire social media career has seemed as dark or inauthentic as people make it seem" — videos framing "what influencers do" as "the most evil thing in the world" are wrong; "it's just reality, it's a great thing." It "changed my life," "changed who I'm building a business with," and "almost everything in my life over the past 4 years would not have been possible without any of this," including the lives of people he has helped. "If you don't have the authority of someone who already has all of the eyes, if you're just starting out and you're using social media as a beginner to build your authority, this is what you're going to have to do."

### U-004-115
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Paying people to share your posts is viable but only works if your content is good — "you're only going to grow if you're good."
- **desarrollo:** "This is why brands pay so much for creators to post about their product because they have an audience." Brands use podcast sponsorships, creator partnerships, Facebook/Google ads, "maybe run ChatGPT ads now," and as a creator "you're practically marketing yourself." "You could technically pay people to share your posts, and that works to some extent if you do it the correct way, but let's not bank on that." For those turned off: "that's a very viable strategy... if you have the money to do it, go ahead. I do not care." Calling it cheating "doesn't make sense... they don't have a business mindset": "people are only going to follow you if your content is good"; if someone shares it and people don't like it, it's wasted money — "the same thing as putting money into Facebook ads, and people not buying your product because your product sucks." "It doesn't really matter if you pay or not, because you're only going to grow if you're good, if you're skilled." Alternative: "if you're smart, you can get in front of other people's audiences for free."
- **ejemplos:** Brand sponsorships; Facebook ads analogy.
- **cita:** "it doesn't really matter if you pay or not, because you're only going to grow if you're good, if you're skilled"

# SECCIÓN 26.5 — Networking: hacer amigos


## CLÚSTER C-T10a-24: Growth is networking (making friends, tribes and masterminds)
- idea: "In reality it's just networking": el crecimiento gratuito "se reduce a hacer amigos", algo que la mayoría no sabe hacer porque, pese a las redes, es antisocial. La gente usa las redes para educarse y entretenerse, sin pensar en hacerse amiga de cuentas de tamaño similar y crecer juntos. Mecanismo: cualquier interacción es un "vote of approval" que te expone a la audiencia de quien interactúa (Vitali: "find your group and grow with it"). Método: insertarse en una tribu con respuestas de calidad a unas 10 cuentas de un círculo hasta que sus consumidores se acostumbren a tu cara; las respuestas "siembran conexiones". Los engagement groups, mal vistos, son en realidad colaboración entre amigos (tareas escolares, socios, un lobby de Call of Duty): "the lone wolf mentality doesn't work"; el autor los llama "mastermind" (Napoleon Hill: "shared minds work better than a singular mind"). Comentar solo para robar seguidores pierde "la parte con sentido". Exige superar la autoimagen de introvertido, escribir como si fuera un chat grupal con amigos, dar sin necesitar (la necesidad se percibe) y arrancar como un lanzamiento de producto: primeros usuarios, composición y boca a boca.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: de "not a huge fan of engagement groups" (2022, en C-T10a-23) a llamarlos mastermind (2023) y "requisito para el éxito" (2024). Ver "Evolución y tensiones".
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

- **ejemplos:** Job opportunity, freelance clients, meeting great people.
- **cita:** "in reality it's just networking"
- **terminos:** growth strategy, networking

Most people in today's world are antisocial. You would think that more people being on social media would be more social, but they're not." So you'll have to talk to people, which on social happens in DMs or replies; one-on-one, in the DMs.

- **desarrollo:** Most people approach social media with the mindset "I want to be educated and entertained." It does not cross their mind that they could reach out and become friends — specifically with people "within a similar follower range as them" — and then grow together, offering value in front of "potentially 4.9 billion people." Dan adds that if you are in "this Creator game or this one person business game" and not on social media, "you've already lost," whether it is paid ads or a personal brand; he recommends a personal brand.

If your content is "authentically good," more people look at you and decide whether to follow or move on. So, besides creating ("content is bread and butter"), there is other work: figure out "who are the people that you associate with," "learn to engage and be present around their content," e.g., commenting as soon as they post, and "find your group and grow with it."

### U-015-118
- **tipo:** método
- **titulo:** Inject yourself in a tribe: quality replies to ~10 great accounts in a circle so its consumers get used to your face; replies seed connections
- **desarrollo:** "Reply to large accounts" is "half-baked" advice. What you actually do: (1) write quality replies — not "great post" or ChatGPT restatements — so people have a reason to visit your profile and follow; apply the 10 commandments to all writing, replies included. (2) Become well known to a tribe: to break into e.g. the startup or tech circle, reply to 10 great accounts there "by adding value and personal experience"; consumers get used to your face and follow "after they see enough, not after my first reply." People unconsciously map who's friends with whom to determine who's important in a network; you must become part of those connections consistently, which takes time. (3) Replies are "seeding connections": the more you reply and DM within a niche group, the easier it is to DM them or have them DM you; they engage more and "feed you opportunities."
- **ejemplos:** Startup circle, tech circle.
- **cita:** "you're seeding connections"
- **terminos:** inject yourself in a tribe, seeding connections

- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-015-115

### U-004-163
- **tipo:** concepto
- **titulo:** Strategy 4, the mastermind: every creator is in group chats that share strategy and each other's posts — "shared minds work better than a singular mind" (Napoleon Hill).
- **desarrollo:** "Harsh truth: every creator you know is in a group chat with other creators." They talk strategy, have fun building businesses, share and help each other grow, and "use the traffic mechanisms to create a strategy they can bake into their routine" — some comment on each other's posts every morning, others repost what's shared in the group. "Most of all they form a tribe and start to identify as a part of a group"; you can see "tribes of 5 to 10 creators that all talk to each other online." People call them "engagement groups"; Dan calls them "a mastermind because that is a pattern in reality: shared minds work better than a singular mind." He attributes the concept to Napoleon Hill ("I believe, I think that was him"): "a shared mind working towards a singular goal." It needn't be "superficial transactions"; you can meet "lifelong friends." If a group is just "you share this post I'll share this post" and you don't care about anyone, "leave, go and form a new one... iterate on everything in business."
- **ejemplos:** Morning comment routines; repost groups; tribes of 5-10.
- **cita:** "shared minds work better than a singular mind"

- **desarrollo:** Engagement groups have become "the weird child of the creator economy," seen as bad. Zoom out: in school you worked with friends on homework and strategized to achieve a goal; in business you find partners, reach out to your network for advice, and help each other grow. That's what a "quote unquote engagement group" is. Analogy: going into a Call of Duty lobby, arguing against the other tribe, communicating and helping your team do better. It doesn't have to be shallow: "a requirement for your success not only online but in any area of your life is to make friends that you work with to grow whatever it is that you're doing." "The Lone Wolf mentality doesn't work in a social setting."

## CLÚSTER C-T10a-25: DMs (how you get in front of people with no audience)
- idea: Sin audiencia, los DMs son cómo te pones frente a la gente y haces conexiones (ofertas de trabajo, tweets de cuentas pequeñas que se viralizan porque un amigo grande los compartió). Dos malentendidos: no ver el poder del efecto red de escribir con constancia y no saber que, con estrategia, cuentas grandes comparten tu post. Reglas: nunca enviar solo "hi", "¿me sigues de vuelta?", un pitch en frío o un bloque de texto; las cuentas grandes reciben 10+ DMs por hora y los hojean como líneas de asunto, así que la vista previa debe capturar atención. Hábito: escribir a alguien cada día y agradecer posts y respuestas que te gustan. Caso: Dickie Bush le escribió a Dan en 2021 continuando un tweet suyo y cinco días después le envió un hilo que Dan compartió ("a smart social game"). Aunque parezca intangible por no dar feedback inmediato, es "the greatest advantage you can have". Los DMs tienen rendimientos decrecientes según el pago: rinden más vendiendo un servicio high ticket que un producto barato o "una impresión", aunque un post también es una oferta cuya conversión es el engagement.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna.
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-015-016
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** When you have no audience, DMs are how you get in front of people; the two misunderstandings about DMs
- **desarrollo:** Everyone starts without an audience, and "DMs are how you get in front of people and you start making connections." Freelancers understand this for landing clients, but the average person trying to grow on social media does not. Dan has seen people land job offers (so this applies to anyone advancing a career, not only business) and seen low-follower tweets go viral because the author was friends with a large account that shared them. People fall into two camps: (1) they do not understand "the sheer power and network effect of dming people on a consistent basis"; (2) they do not understand that "you can get large accounts to share your post if you are strategic enough."
- **ejemplos:** Job offers obtained via DMs; viral tweets from low-follower accounts shared by friends with large followings.
- **cita:** "DMS are how you get in front of people"
- **terminos:** network effect
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** ninguno
- **fuente:** The Non-Needy Networking Process (How To Make High Value Connections).md, 10:34-11:16, 2023-02-12
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-015-017
- **tipo:** advertencia
- **titulo:** Never send DMs that are just "hi", "can you follow back", a cold sales pitch, or a long unspaced block of text
- **desarrollo:** "From here on out never ever ... send a DM that" (1) starts and ends with "hi" or "hey how are you"; (2) asks "can you follow me back"; (3) is a straight sales pitch with no prior connection; (4) is a long block of text that takes too much time to read, especially if the sentences are not spaced out. You must approach DMs tactically, especially with big accounts that get 10+ DMs an hour.
- **ejemplos:** "hi", "hey how are you", "can you follow me back".
- **cita:** ninguna
- **terminos:** ninguno
- **origen:** propia

### U-015-018
- **tipo:** heurística
- **titulo:** Large accounts skim DMs like subject lines: capture attention with what they can see, because replies compound
- **desarrollo:** Dan gets 10+ DMs every hour on almost every platform and physically cannot reply to all. Answering is not one DM back: it is usually a question, he answers, and "the DMs compound," so if he started answering, the number would go from 10+ to 40+ per hour. People with large followings are "just skimming through DMs to see if they're missing anything important," so your DM works "like an email subject line or a hook for a piece of content or a headline for an article": you must capture attention with what they can see in the preview and be strategic about what you say.
- **ejemplos:** 10+ DMs/hour rising to 40+ if he answered; analogy to subject line, hook, headline.
- **cita:** "it's like an email subject line or a hook for a piece of content or a headline for an article"

- **desarrollo:** You should be reaching out to people every single day. - **desarrollo:** In 2021 Dickie Bush, who had many followers, reached out after Dan posted his fitness progress about switching back to high carb to see its impact on mental clarity. Dickie asked questions, shared what he was doing, and the conversation flowed naturally. Mechanism: he took what Dan wrote publicly and "acted as if I was talking to him," continuing the conversation in DMs by linking the tweet and writing his thoughts. Five days later he sent Dan a thread: "threw this together after a bit of reflection, appreciate you bringing the positive energy around here." Dan, who had a decent following, reposted it — and Dickie knew he would. "He's just playing a smart social game and there's nothing wrong with that; that's what we're all doing when we're making friends."

### U-004-156

- **titulo:** DM effort has diminishing returns by payoff: best for high-ticket services; a post is also an offer whose conversion is engagement.

Selling a $2,500 service with a decent number of DMs beats sending the same DMs to sell a $150 product, which beats sending them for "one impression on my post" — "if I treat my post as my offer in the DMs because technically a post is an offer... and the conversion of that offer is engagement."

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-19: The audience as an ecosystem (shared purpose, not every follower is equal)
- idea: Lo que frena a los creadores es una "mindset and awareness gap": pasar del "me me me" de la supervivencia a ver la plataforma como un ecosistema de personas reales que quieren sentirse vistas y escuchadas, y en el que los creadores pueden trabajar juntos hacia una misión (John Hugh, Stan, con la analogía de Maslow). No todo seguidor vale lo mismo: por algunos creadores la gente "daría la vida" y de otros no recuerda ni el nombre. El autor lo enlaza con el mastermind de Napoleon Hill: tu audiencia es una comunidad con un propósito compartido que lo alcanza diez veces más rápido, y tú te vuelves su educador.
- nivel: intermedio
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-004-021
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** What stops creators is a "mindset and awareness gap": moving from "me me me" survival to seeing the platform as an ecosystem of real humans.
- **desarrollo:** John frames it as "the journey of a human being," like "climbing up Maslow's hierarchy of needs": first we're in silos, "me me me," because "I literally need to put food on the table." Then with some success you notice people leaving kind comments (and a couple of mean ones), you feel grateful, want them "to feel seen" — "that's just relationship building in general" — and discover other humans who care about the same things, which brings "giddy, almost childhood joy." Next level of awareness (which can be "utilitarian"): "a more effective business is one where more people are more bought into what you're doing"; you realize "this whole Instagram app is actually an ecosystem" in which creators can work together toward a mission. Also: "every follower isn't worth the same." Conclusion: "you're not just sending your content into the void, there are real human beings on the other end," and the more they feel "seen and heard" and part of something greater, the more effective you are, "purely rationally."
- **ejemplos:** "The ecosystem of Dan" with other successful creators; Emma Chamberlain (see U-004-022).
- **cita:** "you're not just sending your content into the void there are real human beings on the other end"
- **terminos:** mindset and awareness gap; ecosystem; feel seen and heard
- **origen:** de-tercero:John Hugh (Stan co-founder), adaptada-de:Maslow (hierarchy of needs, used as analogy)
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-020
- **fuente:** How To Grow An Audience With Zero Followers & Build A Profitable Startup.md, 20:25-23:01, 2025-01-28
- **tension:** ninguna

- **desarrollo:** Illustrating that follower depth matters more than count, John says some creators have people who would "die for" them; he would for Emma Chamberlain because "she's built such a great community and built such a great brand," but he is also subscribed to "like 17 other people that I can't even name off the top of my head because they're not as personally relevant to me."

### U-008-013
- **tipo:** fuente-de-tercero
- **titulo:** Napoleon Hill's mastermind: a group with a shared purpose actualizes it ten times faster; your audience is that community
- **desarrollo:** Second framing point: we need more holistic solutions for a community with a shared purpose. Napoleon Hill's concept of a mastermind is, in essence, a group of two or more people with a shared purpose who, working together, can actualize that purpose "10 times faster than they would be able to alone." Dan's adaptation: you build a community of people with the same purpose; what you attract people to is your vision or purpose for the future; by combining minds you help others hit it much faster. Your audience is the community you serve, and in the creator economy each member will have their own community if they lean into their entrepreneurial nature. You distill your knowledge, expertise, experience and skill set to them to reach the goals you set for them — you become an educator.
- **ejemplos:** Napoleon Hill's mastermind.
- **cita:** "by working together they can actualize that purpose 10 times faster than they would be able to alone"
- **terminos:** mastermind; shared purpose; Holistic Solutions; educator
- **origen:** adaptada-de:Napoleon Hill
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-008-012
- **fuente:** The Future Of One-Person Businesses (Take Advantage Now).md, 8:30-9:20, 2023-03-12
- **tension:** ninguna



## CLÚSTER C-T10b-37: Networking is making friends (the tribe)
- idea: El networking (mejor llamado "making friends") es "el pilar" del éxito del autor y, según él, de cualquier creador. El mundo digital no tiene barreras: antes el acceso dependía de la universidad, la ubicación y el estatus (los introvertidos "no tenían chance"); hoy el éxito depende de con quién estás conectado (el "mastermind" online: meta, negocio y escritura, en ese orden). El objetivo son amigos reales que trabajan hacia metas similares, se apoyan y comparten estrategias; con cinco amigos, un gran post se comparte con solo pedirlo y tú devuelves el favor (value exchange). En sus comienzos en Twitter no respondía por responder: formaba una tribu unida, lo que la mayoría de los creadores pasa por alto. Su propia historia: seis meses de lurking hasta preguntarse "¿por qué solo consumo?", Jose Rosado, Joey Justice y la mudanza a Texas.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: ninguna
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

- **ejemplos:** See the Jose Rosado, Joey Justice and Texas move units.

### U-015-006

- **titulo:** "The digital world has no barriers": the evolution of networking from college/location/status to the internet
- **desarrollo:** Dan shows a graphic (not reconstructible beyond what he narrates) illustrating "the evolution of networking." Twenty years ago you needed college, network, location, profession and status to get opportunity; today "all you really need is the internet." In the past the only way to the top was your network, and your network was limited by your physical location or by the prestigious college you attended and by climbing the ladder. Introverts "didn't stand a chance" — Dan says he would have been out of a job. Today you can DM a celebrity, and if you are a good persuasive writer (a skill) "there is a string of words that you can say that will get them to respond." He repeats the phrase at the end of the video as "such an important thing": we do not live in the world of 10 years ago.
- **ejemplos:** DMing a celebrity; the graphic comparing old and new networking requirements.

- **titulo:** Self-confidence step 4: make new friends online (the mastermind); goal, business and writing in that order
- **desarrollo:** "There is a concept called a mastermind: multiple minds working together to achieve a shared goal." In the past you needed local connections or a prestigious local school; "now you have access to anyone at the click of a button. Your success is no longer determined by your socioeconomic status and physical location; it is determined by who you are connected to and the opportunities they can pass off." How to find connections: have a goal; attract people by starting a business with a mission to achieve that goal; get in front of well-connected people by writing in public. Link to confidence: if you don't push beyond limits, fail and overcome, you won't become confident; "a goal, business and writing in that order are how you continue to repeat this process to infinity." "There are no limits except for the ones you place on yourself"; in a job "someone is in charge of the limits you can reach." If you don't hop "on the evolution train with technology, AI and the internet," they will make you obsolete. You make friends online "by just showing up and talking to people," like at a party; online you increase social proof with your brand and ideas, offline status with cars, looks and material acquisitions, "and it's obvious which one leads to a better life."
- **ejemplos:** ninguno
- **cita:** "your success is no longer determined by your socioeconomic status and physical location it is determined by who you are connected to"
- **terminos:** mastermind; social proof; evolution train
- **origen:** adaptada-de:the mastermind concept (no author named)
- **nivel:** intermedio
- **prerrequisitos:** U-018-094
- **fuente:** It’s actually pretty easy to be self-disciplined (if you stop getting distracted).md, 22:03-24:12, 2024-05-12
- **tension:** ninguna


### U-004-119
- **tipo:** principio
- **titulo:** The goal is actual friends who support each other; with five friends, a great post can be shared on request — and you return the favor (value exchange).
- **desarrollo:** "The goal here is to make actual friends, because what do friends do? They support each other, and they're usually working toward the same goal. They have a similar value set." With five people you're in communication with, you write a great piece and send it: "Hey, I put a lot of time into this... I think your audience will resonate with it. Can you share it?" It takes time to get there, "but it really does become that simple once you actually have friends," since sharing takes no effort. Condition: "if the content sucks, then they're not going to want to do that. So, go back to step one." And "you're there to return the favor": with Eden, he asked friends if they liked it, asked them to promote the launch, and said "Anytime that you launch product or anytime you need something shared, please do not hesitate to send it to me, so I can return the favor. Value exchange."
- **ejemplos:** Eden launch promoted by friends with large followings.
- **cita:** "what do friends do? They support each other, and they're usually working toward the same goal."
- **terminos:** value exchange; return the favor
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-004-116

### U-015-005
- **tipo:** historia
- **titulo:** Discovering Jose Rosado's tweet and six months of lurking led to the realization "why am I just consuming?"
- **desarrollo:** When Dan returned to Twitter, a tweet from Jose Rosado popped up: a "heavy hitting truth," a "harsh truth" ("stop being a little [expletive] and go do this"), the kind of content Dan loves. He followed him and realized Rosado talked about both self-improvement and online business, Dan's two interests. Following him exposed more accounts through retweets and replies (the "network effect"), and "before I knew it I was drowning in high value Money Twitter advice." As an introvert he lurked without engaging, commenting or doing anything for about six months. Then it "hit me all at once": all of these people were writing the same tweets he could be writing; he enjoyed the content because they were confirming his beliefs; and they were clearly getting leads, followers, sales and income. A "little mindset shift or shift in perception" — seeing from the lens of "why am I not doing the same exact thing" — "changed the direction of my life."
- **ejemplos:** Jose Rosado's harsh-truth tweets; Money Twitter; six months of lurking.
- **cita:** "why am I just sitting here consuming when I could be doing the same exact thing"

## CLÚSTER C-T10b-38: Non-needy networking (the seven-step process)
- idea: Proceso de siete pasos, repetible, para entablar amistad con otros creadores sin necesidad ni pedidos prematuros: el valor y la relación van antes que cualquier pedido, que llega al final. Su propósito es poner tu nombre "frente a los ojos y en la boca de la gente" y que, por ejemplo, una cuenta de 50.000 seguidores comparta tu post sin depender del algoritmo. Pasos (2023 / 2026): (1) encontrar a quién escribir (alguien que de verdad quieras, un pequeño grupo curado para crecer juntos 6–12 meses; minar la lista de "following"); (2) inspired compliment / simple praise; (3) mostrar interés genuino ("showing interest makes you interesting"), sobre todo por lo que construyen; (4) lead with value / show you're useful (no necesitas valor propio, basta conectarlos con un recurso o una persona; reciprocidad); (5) llamada opcional; (6) seguir aportando valor y mantener una "faint connection"; (7) pedir (group chat, preguntas, compartir un post bueno; "brutal honesty"). Escribe como a un amigo (gimnasio), no como un robot corporativo; las habilidades sociales son quizá la habilidad más importante en redes. Plazo: 5 DMs al día durante 30–60 días; los resultados se acumulan en 2 semanas a 3 meses.
- nivel: fundamental
- evolución: renombres y matices entre 2023 y 2026: "inspired compliment" → "simple praise"; "lead with value" → "show you're useful" (conectar con valor, no tenerlo); "follow up with value" → "faint connection"; "follow up with an ask" → "ask" con "brutal honesty"; se añaden el grupo curado de 6–12 meses y el umbral de 5 DMs/día. La heurística de 2023 de "subir la escalera" (U-015-022) se matiza con el paid growth como filtro para cuentas grandes (U-004-161).
- fusiones (unidades sustancialmente idénticas; usa la unión de su contenido): ninguna

### U-015-019
- **tipo:** framework
- **titulo:** The seven steps to non-needy networking (overview, origin and purpose)
- **desarrollo:** Dan presents "the seven steps to non-needy networking." Origin: it started as a guide in Digital Economics, was repurposed into 2 Hour Writer to help people get eyes on their writing, and put into Modern Mastery; it is "foundational for anyone trying to grow business or just improve their career." The seven steps: (1) find somebody you want to DM; (2) send an inspired compliment; (3) show genuine interest in them; (4) lead with value; (5) get on a call to make a deeper connection (optional); (6) follow up with value; (7) follow up with an ask. Plus a "bonus tip number eight" about time. Purpose when "peeled back": to get your name "in front of people's eyes and in their mouths." He clarifies the order is variable: "these are kind of just the principles," rooted in influence/persuasion. Why "non-needy" (inferred from the sequence, not explicitly defined): value and relationship come before any ask, and the ask comes only at the end.
- **ejemplos:** Each step is developed in the following units.
- **cita:** "get your name in front of people's eyes and in their mouths"
- **terminos:** non-needy networking, seven steps to non-needy networking
- **origen:** propia
- **nivel:** fundamental
- **prerrequisitos:** U-015-016
- **fuente:** The Non-Needy Networking Process (How To Make High Value Connections).md, 12:48-13:34, 21:10, 2023-02-12
- **tension:** ninguna

- **tipo:** fuente-de-tercero

- **desarrollo:** Dan says the steps are variable and "just the principles," and that if you study influence or persuasion you'll understand them better; he recommends "the book influence by Robert Cialdini." He also invokes "the Law of reciprocity" in step 2. He does not elaborate further on Cialdini's content.

### U-015-020
- **tipo:** argumento
- **titulo:** Outreach is not a one-off cycle: kind, valuable outreach keeps you top of mind for referrals
- **desarrollo:** Even freelancers think they are in a constant cycle of "I need to reach out to this guy and then the conversation ends if they never respond." Dan says no: if you reach out to 100 people, are kind, have value to offer, and walk through the process, then even if they cannot help you directly, they will have you "top of mind for the next person that comes to them and asks if they know anyone" who can help with web design, email marketing, fitness, etc.
- **ejemplos:** Referrals for web design, email marketing, fitness.
- **cita:** "they're gonna have you top of mind for the next person"
